**(iii) No defense exists inside the window, and this is the sharpest departure from F-01's disposition.** The same `VotingWeight` invariant that makes the Gauge branch accidentally safe forces every `getPastVotes(holder, snapshotBlock)` read to zero whenever the total is zero, so no honest party can cast an against-vote at any weight. F-01 was bounded to Medium in part by the observation that one larger honest against-vote defeats the attacker; that mitigation cannot fire here, because the single condition that vacates the denominator also empties every ballot that might oppose it. F-06 froze the denominator as a historical read, so no post-snapshot poke moves it either. The contest window remains open and nothing can be done inside it.

**(iv) The sequence, its cost, and its fit inside the window.** `GaugeEligibility.meetsCompositionQualityGate` L385-L392 is a static structural check — the pool's hook must be the fee-routing hook, the pool must come from the approved factory, and the 52 percent weight numerator must clear `0.52e18` — with no TVL floor, no age requirement and no EMA dependency, so a pool deployed on day 0 passes it on day 0. `proposeCompositionChallenge` requires no voting weight, only the non-refundable 1,000 svZCHF deposit donated to der Bodensee. `VOTING_DELAY_BLOCKS` 7,200 plus `VOTING_PERIOD_BLOCKS` 100,800 plus `EXECUTION_TIMELOCK_BLOCKS` 14,400 is 122,400 blocks, roughly 17 days, which fits inside the 432,000-block maturity window three times over. `queue` and `execute` are both permissionless, so the attacker drives the entire sequence alone, and `_executeProposal` L362-L367 then revokes the incumbent's gauge, calls `replaceSlot`, and gauges the challenger. Nothing serialises proposals, so all 26 filled slots are available in parallel for 26,000 svZCHF.

**(v) There is no administrative remedy, because the slot registry's governance was handed over at Stage K.** `script/DeployStageK.s.sol` L159 calls `setGovernanceContract(address(governance))` on `MILIARIUM_REGISTRY`, which is how the composition path reaches `replaceSlot` at all; the bind is part of the Stage F through K stack already broadcast to Sepolia in phase 4, not a dissolution-time rotation, and `MiliariumRegistry` is deliberately absent from PB2.6's five-rotation roster. Since `replaceSlot` and `setGovernanceContract` are both `onlyGovernance` on that registry, once the bind lands the multisig can neither re-slot directly nor rotate the role back. The only available response is another composition challenge on identical terms. That bounds permanence, since the honest side can restore a slot for its own deposit and its own 17 days, but it restores no defense: inside the window slot occupancy belongs to whoever proposed most recently, and the captured slot earns emissions throughout each cycle.

**(vi) Severity High and disposition FIX, both user-adjudicated at PB3.8q.** High rather than Medium because the defense is structurally unavailable rather than merely requiring vigilance, and because no governance weight whatsoever is needed to propose, where F-01 at least required a single qualified holder. High rather than Critical because LP principal and the Vault substrate are untouched, no funds are stolen, the sequence is loud on chain and takes 17 days to complete, and restoration is symmetric on identical terms. Accepted-risk was considered and declined: an operational mitigation that delayed the Stage K registry bind past EMA maturity would close the launch window and leave the mid-life path at (vii) open.

**(vii) The fix sits at the denominator, not at the branch.** `_voteSucceeded` reads the snapshot supply once into a local, returns false when that local is zero, and only then performs the existing basis-point comparison against it. The guard closes both branches at once; it makes the Gauge and Fee branches' safety local to the tally instead of derived from a cross-contract invariant; and it covers a re-entry path that no launch sequencing could reach, in which an attacker permissionlessly pokes every holder down to zero under a protocol-wide EMA staleness and re-opens the window mid-life. That mid-life path is reasoned from `poke`'s permissionlessness and the `EMA_STALENESS_BLOCKS` short-circuit and is recorded here as an open assessment rather than a demonstrated vector; it is not PoC'd. The belt-and-braces alternative of also rejecting a zero `totalVotes` was considered and declined as redundant, since a non-zero supply forces `totalVotes` above the 20 percent quorum floor before either majority branch is evaluated, leaving the `>=` form sound as written.

**(viii) Blast radius, sized at pre-flight per the I13 lesson.** `test/unit/AureumGovernance.t.sol` is unaffected: its `setUp` sets a non-zero supply at L157, so every tally in that suite already runs against a populated denominator. The two mocks that hardcode `getPastTotalSupply` to zero, `test/fork/mocks/StageGMocks.sol` L57 and `test/unit/VaultClassRegistry.t.sol` L52, feed other consumers and appear in no AureumGovernance tally path. `F06_postEndBlockDenominatorFreeze.t.sol` uses a block-aware mock returning 1,000,000e18. The derivation of a non-existent proposal stays `Defeated` because `GaugeChallenge` is the zero value of `ProposalType` and the zero proposal therefore takes the strict branch. The PB3.8p PoC inverts by design and becomes the regression test, which is the intended change and not breakage. The three fork suites that reach a tally, `StageKIntegration`, `StageOIntegration` and `StagePIntegration`, all establish weight before doing so, but whether each fixture's poke clears the 60-day maturity gate cannot be settled by reading and is the named exit gate for the fix.

## PB-D63 — PB3.6b: F-22, the post-Stage-K Vault admin surface is permitted to governance and reachable by no caller, so every emergency lever is a one-way door; severity Critical, disposition FIX in source for Stage R (2026-08-05; PB3.6b)

**(i) What the PB3.6b-F22a PoC established.** `test/fork/F22_vaultAdminSurfaceUnreachable.t.sol` separates permission from expressibility across three fork-green faces. The premise face asserts `AureumGovernanceAuthorizer.canPerform` L65-L73 returns true for `GOVERNANCE_CONTRACT` on an arbitrary actionId and on the zero actionId alike, without exception. The dispositive face pranks the governance contract as `msg.sender` and watches the Vault ACCEPT `setProtocolFeeController`, mutating state that no on-chain transaction could ever produce. The third face pranks the in-fork multisig and takes `SenderNotAllowed`. The defect is therefore not a missing grant: the grant is total, and what is missing is any code path able to emit the call. `AureumGovernance.execute()` at `src/governance/AureumGovernance.sol` L343-L374 dispatches over exactly three proposal types to three fixed targets, and the `Proposal` struct carries no target field and no calldata field, so no proposal can name a function outside that enumeration.

**(ii) The enumeration, which is what makes this systemic rather than a single stranded lever.** `VaultAdmin.sol` carries eleven `authenticate`-gated entries: `pauseVault`, `unpauseVault`, `setProtocolFeeController`, `enableRecoveryMode`, `disableRecoveryMode`, `disableQuery`, `disableQueryPermanently`, `enableQuery`, `pauseVaultBuffers`, `unpauseVaultBuffers`, `setAuthorizer`. Nine are reachable by no caller at all. The two exceptions are `pauseVault` and `enableRecoveryMode`, reachable only by `EMERGENCY_MULTISIG`, only while `block.number < EMERGENCY_WINDOW_END_BLOCK`, because `_isEmergencyAction` L75-L77 admits exactly those two actionIds.

**(iii) The surviving two are both entries, and both inverses are among the nine.** `pauseVault` is reachable and `unpauseVault` is not; `enableRecoveryMode` is reachable and `disableRecoveryMode` is not. That is the whole finding in one line: every lever that remains is a door that only opens inward. An emergency mechanism whose exits are unreachable is not a degraded emergency mechanism, it is a trap, and it is armed by the party the design most trusts.

**(iv) The two entered states differ in stickiness, and the worse one compounds with a permissionless step.** A Vault pause self-clears: `VaultCommon._isVaultPaused` L149-L152 gates on `block.timestamp <= _vaultBufferPeriodEndTime`, so with the Stage-A row-7 four-year window plus six-month buffer an unpausable pause lifts at roughly deploy plus four and a half years. Recovery mode does not self-clear; it is a pool config bit cleared only by `disableRecoveryMode`, which has no caller, so it is permanent. And `enableRecoveryMode` L346-L356 skips `_authenticateCaller()` entirely when the pool or the Vault is already paused, so once anything is paused ANY anonymous address may place ANY pool into permanent recovery mode. The pause precondition does not even require the emergency window: pool `pauseManager` is the governance multisig and `VaultAdmin._ensureAuthenticatedByRole` L787-L793 is NON-exclusive, so the multisig may pause individual Miliarium pools directly through the role at any time. Pools in recovery mode permit proportional exit only, so LP principal stays recoverable while swaps, fees, emissions and CCB scoring stop for good.

**(v) The window bounds entry but not the state entered, which defeats naive symmetry.** `EMERGENCY_WINDOW_END_BLOCK` is fixed at authorizer construction as `block.number + EMERGENCY_WINDOW_BLOCKS`. A pause or a recovery-mode entry on the last block of that window outlives it — the pause to the Vault's buffer end, the recovery bit forever. A fix that grants exit on the SAME window as entry is therefore still broken at the edge: it permits entering a permanent state with zero blocks remaining in which to leave it. Any exit authority must either outlive the entry authority or be backed by a second, permanent path.

**(vi) Severity Critical, recorded with its reasoning so the call does not drift.** The distinguishing property is that the trigger is CORRECT USE rather than misuse. F-21 required an attacker to act deliberately and spend a 1,000 svZCHF deposit; this requires only that an incident occur and that a responder do exactly what the mechanism exists for. Pausing during a genuine emergency is the intended behaviour, and it is the act that permanently disables every pool recovery mode subsequently touches, with the escalating step available to anonymous callers and no on-chain actor able to reverse any of it. Weighed against Critical: no funds are stolen and proportional exit survives, so custody is intact and the loss is the protocol's function rather than its users' balances. Weighed for: the outcome is permanent, protocol-wide, reachable without an adversary, and unreversible by every party including the one that caused it. The permanence and the correct-use trigger carry it past High.

**(vii) Prior art, and the boundary of what is new here.** That `AureumGovernance` v1 exposes no generic-call hatch is already on record at K-D9, at PB-D12 (ii), which triages the stranded Aureum-owned setters, and at PB-D61 / RB-017 for the hook's `governanceModule`. Every one of those concerns a contract gated by its own `governanceContract` slot. What no document enumerates is the VAULT's own admin surface, reached instead through the authorizer, which the identical fixed dispatch strands identically. Three live artifacts presuppose the opposite and are wrong post-K: `docs/STAGE_A_PLAN.md` row 4 states the Vault admin surface is gated by the authorizer and governable through it, true for Stages A-K and silently false thereafter; `src/vault/AureumVaultFactory.sol` L28 NatSpec says the Vault's controller may diverge if governance later calls `Vault.setProtocolFeeController`, a call no governance can make; and `test/fork/DissolutionGovernanceHandoff.t.sol` L142's NatSpec asserts `AureumGovernance` holds pause via the authorizer regardless, which is permission-true and expressibility-false. All three want correcting at the fix.

**(viii) The keystone, and why `setAuthorizer`-only does not self-close.** Every `authenticate` call resolves through `_authorizer.canPerform`, so `setAuthorizer` is the one lever that redefines all the others after deployment; leaving it unreachable makes every permission decision taken at Stage R correct-or-stranded on first deploy, forever. Restoring it is therefore ranked first. But restoring it ALONE is insufficient, and the reason is circular in a way worth writing down: governance installs a new authorizer, that authorizer grants some principal, and the principal must still be able to EXPRESS the call. Grant it to `AureumGovernance` and F-22 reproduces exactly. Grant it to a fresh contract able to make arbitrary Vault calls and that is a generic hatch under another name, owed its own threat model. So the constrained type must carry the exits as well as the keystone.

**(ix) The fix is two-layered, because a proposal can never be an emergency response.** `VOTING_DELAY_BLOCKS` 7,200 plus `VOTING_PERIOD_BLOCKS` 100,800 plus `EXECUTION_TIMELOCK_BLOCKS` 14,400 is 122,400 blocks, about seventeen days, with no expedited path — de-escalation latency, not incident latency. Layer one, the authorizer: add `unpauseVault` and `disableRecoveryMode` to the emergency action set so the emergency principal's in-window response is not one-way, with the exit grant expiring on the SAME window as entry per (x). Layer two, `AureumGovernance`: one constrained proposal type whose targets are exactly `setAuthorizer`, `unpauseVault` and `disableRecoveryMode` — named targets, no calldata field, no generic dispatch — so that after the window the protocol can still de-escalate and still migrate its own permission map, by vote, with no standing key. Seventeen days is acceptable for leaving an emergency state and unacceptable for entering one, which is precisely why the two layers hold different halves.

**(x) The exit tail dies with entry, which is safe only BECAUSE of layer two.** The emergency exit grant expires at `EMERGENCY_WINDOW_END_BLOCK` alongside the entry grant, the most §xxix-conservative reading: the constitution admits an emergency role for `BLOCKS_PER_YEAR` blocks and then kills the clause permanently, and a standing exit-only key past that date fights it. The (v) edge case is answered at the protocol level rather than by extending the key, since layer two can express `unpauseVault` and `disableRecoveryMode` permanently by vote. This clause is load-bearing in one direction only: die-with-entry MUST NOT be adopted without layer two, or a month-eleven recovery mode becomes permanent exactly as it is today. A short exit-only authorizer tail past entry remains available as an optimization and is explicitly not required.

**(xi) Disposition, and what this lock does not do.** FIX in source, landing for the Stage-R mainnet deploy; Sepolia is NOT remediated, by decision rather than omission — the deployed contracts are immutable, the finding is exactly what a testnet exists to surface, and this is the disposition shape PB-D52 (viii) already set when it declined the ten-contract cascade and left the live oracle pre-fix. The CircuitBreaker architecture is DESCOPED to its own track and must not gate Stage R: predicate design, the L2 soft-brake layer over Aureum-owned emission and gauge surfaces, cold-start behaviour below the 432,000-block EMA maturity, and a full §xxix read on who may hold emergency authority after dissolution all belong there. F-22's own scope is expressibility of the exits plus the keystone, nothing more. No code lands from this lock; the build ladder and its blast-radius sizing are the next rungs.

## PB-D64 — PB3.6c: the F-22 build shape, settling the typed-domain questions PB-D63 left open and amending its one-proposal-type wording to three (2026-08-05; PB3.6c)

**(i) Three proposal types, not one, which amends PB-D63 (ix).** That clause specified "one constrained proposal type whose targets are exactly `setAuthorizer`, `unpauseVault` and `disableRecoveryMode`"; the build shape is three types, `VaultAuthorizerChange`, `VaultUnpause` and `VaultRecoveryDisable`, and PB-D63 (ix) is amended to read that way. The rejected alternative was one type carrying a sub-enum naming which of the three, which fails on two counts: it introduces a second dispatch level inside a single `_executeProposal` branch, where the contract's existing shape is one proposal type to one code path with no nesting anywhere; and it forces `_voteSucceeded` to branch on the sub-type the moment the majorities differ, which they do per (iv). A sub-selector is also the nearest thing to the calldata field PB-D63 (ix) explicitly excluded, so three named types is the reading that honours its own constraint.

**(ii) The new values append, and the reason is weaker than it first appears but still binding.** Enum values are positional, so stored proposals and every external decoder of `getProposal` key on the index; inserting anywhere but the end reinterprets records already written. The secondary reason, that appending avoids re-pointing the zero value, is worth stating precisely rather than overstating: F-21's guard means a non-existent proposal now derives to `Defeated` through the quorum test rather than through the branch, since zero votes fail `totalVotes * 10_000 < supply * QUORUM_BPS` at any non-zero supply, so `GaugeChallenge` occupying slot zero is no longer solely load-bearing for that path. Append anyway, on the storage-and-decoder ground, which holds independently.

**(iii) Payload placement, with one new field and one deliberate reuse.** `Proposal` gains exactly one member, `address newAuthorizer`, read only by `VaultAuthorizerChange`. `VaultRecoveryDisable` carries its pool in the existing `targetPool`, which is semantically exact and needs no field. `VaultUnpause` carries no payload at all and leaves every payload member zero. The struct grows from thirteen members to fourteen, appended last; per the I12 lesson the consumers were enumerated before locking the shape — `_createProposal` writes every member, `_executeProposal` and `_voteSucceeded` read subsets, and `getProposal` returns a copy, so the change is additive in storage and a tuple-width change for any external decoder of `getProposal`, which is the one caller-visible consequence and is named here so it is not discovered later.

**(iv) Majorities differ by direction, deliberately.** `VaultAuthorizerChange` joins the existing `CompositionChallenge` branch at an integer-exact two-thirds supermajority: it is the lever that redefines every permission in the system and it should not pass on a lower bar than swapping a single Miliarium slot. `VaultUnpause` and `VaultRecoveryDisable` take the simple-majority else branch unchanged, because they are de-escalation and it would be perverse to make leaving an emergency state harder than the two-thirds bar that never guarded entering one. The asymmetry is the point rather than an oversight.

**(v) The timelock stays shared at two days.** `queue()` is untouched and `EXECUTION_TIMELOCK_BLOCKS` continues to apply uniformly. The fourteen-day public voting window plus the timelock already yields roughly sixteen days of visibility before any authorizer migration can execute, and a per-type timelock is an available refinement rather than a requirement of this finding. Recorded as explicitly considered and declined so a later reader does not read the uniform timelock as an omission.

**(vi) Propose-time guards on the new authorizer, and why propose-time suffices.** `proposeVaultAuthorizerChange` rejects `address(0)` with the existing `ZeroAddress()` error and rejects any address carrying no code with a new error, because an authorizer that is either would make every `authenticate` call in the Vault revert permanently — a failure strictly worse than the one F-22 documents, and reached by a passing vote rather than by an attack. The value is frozen in the proposal at creation, so a propose-time check is sufficient by the F-20 precedent; unlike the composition gate there is no mutable eligibility surface between propose and execute that could go stale, so no execute-time re-check is added.

**(vii) Layer one lands first and is independent.** `AureumGovernanceAuthorizer` gains two immutable actionIds for `unpauseVault` and `disableRecoveryMode`, computed in the constructor from the same vault disambiguator, and `_isEmergencyAction` widens to four. It shares no file with layer two and gates nothing in it. Its test blast radius carries one trap worth naming: `test_canPerform_emergencyMultisigNonEmergencyAction_false` is a fuzz over `bytes32` that `vm.assume`s away exactly the two current emergency actionIds, so widening the set will not fail it — the odds of the fuzzer producing a specific `bytes32` are negligible — it will simply stop asserting what its name claims. The assume set must grow to four in the same commit, the post-window test should cover the two new actions, and in-window positive cases are new.

**(viii) The F-22 PoC does not invert, which is the structural difference from F-21.** `test/fork/F22_vaultAdminSurfaceUnreachable.t.sol` witnesses `setProtocolFeeController`, and that function stays unreachable after the full fix because PB-D63 (xi) scopes F-22 to the exits plus the keystone and nothing more. The PoC therefore stays green and changes role from proof-of-defect to attestation-of-accepted-residual, and its NatSpec must be rewritten to say so or it will read as a stale failure nobody closed. Proving the fix needs NEW tests asserting the three targets are now reachable end to end. The residual is closable later without reopening this decision: `setAuthorizer` reachability plus a principal able to express arbitrary Vault calls is precisely the CircuitBreaker track PB-D63 (xi) split out.

**(ix) The ladder.** a — the layer-one authorizer change plus its test updates. b — the layer-two enum, struct member, three `propose*` entries, the `_voteSucceeded` majority join and the three `_executeProposal` branches, as one coherent contract change. c — the reachability tests for all three targets plus the PoC NatSpec rewrite. d — the three stale-artifact corrections PB-D63 (vii) names, in `docs/STAGE_A_PLAN.md` row 4, `src/vault/AureumVaultFactory.sol` L28 and `test/fork/DissolutionGovernanceHandoff.t.sol` L142. e — the F-22 ledger row. f — the PB-D63 and PB-D64 Decisions rows and the section 11 update. No rung is a broadcast; Sepolia stays as deployed per PB-D63 (xi).

**(x) The propose-time guard's error name, whose behaviour (vi) locks without ever naming it.** The error added at rung b1 is `AuthorizerNotContract(address newAuthorizer)`, recorded here because until this clause it existed only in the b1 commit. The name follows the contract's established convention of a descriptive condition carrying the offending value as its sole parameter — `InvalidFeeTarget(address pool)` L152, `InvalidGaugeTarget(address pool)` L155, `CompositionQualityGateFailed(address pool)` L157 — rather than a generic `InvalidAuthorizer` or a bare unnamed revert. The zero-address case deliberately does NOT fold into it and keeps the existing `ZeroAddress()`, so the two rejections stay distinguishable at the call site: a zero address is a caller slip the contract already has vocabulary for, whereas a codeless non-zero address is the specific hazard (vi) identifies, an authorizer that would make every `authenticate` call in the Vault revert permanently and is reached by a passing vote rather than by an attack. The signature is locked rather than left to git because an error's parameter shape is a typed-domain artifact — it is part of the ABI and is what an external decoder keys on — so it belongs in the lock beside the behaviour it reports, per the section 12 ambiguity-gate that names error parameter shape explicitly.

## PB-D65 — PB3.7b: the deployed-addresses artifact is a chain-id-keyed JSON product for the OQ-18 frontend rather than more Solidity, the stub topology closes RB-014's one remaining leg on the way, and the Solidity-to-JSON agreement test is an intersection rather than full coverage (user-adjudicated 2026-08-06; PB3.7b)

**(i) What PB3.7a delivered, and why it does not close PB3.7.** `script/config/SepoliaPhase4Addresses.sol` was built to unblock the PB-D59 registry bind, an in-repo Solidity consumer, and its own NatSpec names it the first PB3.7 rung. The step's actual target is the Surfaces-table entry reading "the C-integration contract the frontend consumes", and OQ-18 resolves that consumer as `aumm-app`, a separate TypeScript repo that consumes contract ABIs from `aumm-deploy` as a dependency. A Solidity library is unreadable to it. The remainder is therefore a machine-readable artifact that exists in no form today, `git ls-files` returning zero tracked JSON or TypeScript address files.

**(ii) The inventory, measured rather than recalled.** Fifty-one live protocol contracts across the deployment record: nine base-layer at nonces 90 to 98 in section 1, with the abandoned 87 to 89 trio labelled beside them; twenty-six Miliarium pools, the twenty-five of section 7 plus slot 02's third-sender redeploy in section 10; and sixteen phase-4 in section 11. Exactly sixteen are published, the set `SepoliaPhase4Addresses.sol` carries. The eighty-seven testnet stubs of section 2 live only in the gitignored `.env.sepolia`, which section 2 itself flags as presently unversioned, and `out/` with `out-vault/` are gitignored so no ABI is a committed product either.

**(iii) Format and key: chain id, never chain name.** The artifact is JSON under `deployments/`, keyed by chain id rather than by the string sepolia, because Stage R adds mainnet and a name-keyed file forces a reshape at exactly the moment reshaping is most expensive. Either `deployments/11155111.json` or a single `deployments.json` whose top-level object is keyed by that id; the build rung picks one and states it, and no parallel name key is introduced alongside. This supersedes the drafting shorthand that called the file `deployments/sepolia.json`.

**(iv) The stub set is in scope, and RB-014's remaining gap is narrower than its own row states.** The Sepolia frontend renders pool balances, so it needs the stub tokens the pools actually hold, and those enter the artifact. RB-014's row predates PB-D47's h1 through h8 and now overstates what is open: `_ensureWithRate` at `test-stubs/DeployTestnetStubs.s.sol` L140 to L148 creates three contracts per WITH_RATE token and records one, but the caller emits the rate provider under the disjoint `STUB_RP_` namespace at L131 and five composite providers carry named keys at L193 to L197. What is emitted nowhere is the eighteen-decimal `StubERC20` underlying, created at L142 and handed to the vault at L143 with its address never recorded, recoverable only by an `asset()` probe on the vault, which is the weaker of the two recovery paths RB-014 itself distinguishes.

**(v) RB-014 closes in three parts rather than one.** Copying eighty-seven env lines is the least of it. First, the committed set carries the topology a frontend and an operator need: the pool legs, the WITH_RATE underlyings, and the rate providers where they exist. Second, the script stops under-emitting so a future run cannot recreate the gap, `_ensureWithRate` recording the underlying under a namespace disjoint from both `STUB_` and `STUB_RP_`. Third, those addresses land in the committed deployment record per section 8f and in the artifact derived from it, not in a JSON derived from a still-gitignored `.env`. That third part is why the ladder's c rung precedes d: section 2 carries no machine-readable stub map today, so a generator has nothing to audit against until one exists.

**(vi) The Solidity-to-JSON agreement test is an INTERSECTION, deliberately.** `SepoliaPhase4Addresses.sol` covers sixteen addresses where the JSON carries fifty-one plus the stubs, so a test asserting agreement proves only that the two artifacts concur on the phase-4 set. It is a drift guard over the overlap and not full-set coverage, and it must be named that way or a later reader will mistake a green test for a complete cross-check. The Solidity surface is NOT widened to mirror the JSON, since address libraries for the base layer, the pools and the stubs would add Solidity whose only consumer is that test while the JSON is the canonical product. Widening stays available and is explicitly not done here.

**(vii) Abandoned deployments are carried with an explicit status rather than omitted.** Nonces 87 to 89 hold a superseded `AureumAuthorizer`, `AureumProtocolFeeController` and `AureumVaultFactory`. Section 8f's rule is that abandoned deployments stay in the record under a label rather than being deleted, because a block explorer shows them regardless and a reader must be able to tell which set is live. The artifact therefore carries a status field, so a consumer distinguishes live from superseded without reading the prose record.

**(viii) Derivation is by tool and audited as a diff against the record.** `SepoliaPhase4Addresses.sol` set the precedent, its checksummed forms being `cast to-check-sum-address` output over section 11's own lowercase literals rather than hand-cased, so it derives from the record by tool and its audit is a diff against that derivation. The JSON follows the same rule, generated from the deployment record and audited by diffing the generator's output against that record rather than against any expected-file typed from a reading, per the §8e.1 transcription rule.

**(ix) ABIs are OUT of scope and belong to PB4.1.** OQ-18's dependency sentence names ABIs beside addresses, but the plan splits them: PB3.7 is the addresses artifact and PB4.1 hands that artifact to `aumm-app`. Which ABI subset the MVP needs is a frontend-side question, and `out/` being gitignored makes publishing them its own decision between a committed ABI package and a documented `forge build` export. Packaging ABIs here would widen a step whose gate is the artifact's existence.

**(x) The ladder.** b this lock. c the RB-014 closure, the stub topology into the committed deployment record plus the script emission fix. d the generator script. e the artifact itself. f the intersection agreement test. g the ledger row, the Completion Log and the section 11 update. c precedes d because the generator audits against a record that must first carry the stub map.

**(xi) The artifact's file shape and the generator's language, home and invocation, closing the pick clause (iii) deferred to this rung.** The artifact is `deployments/11155111.json`, ONE FILE PER CHAIN rather than one file keyed by chain: Stage R then adds a sibling `deployments/1.json` with no merge surface against Sepolia's, per-chain diffs stay scoped to the chain that changed, and each file is independently auditable against its own deployment record. Its schema carries `chainId` at top level, then `baseLayer`, `pools` keyed by Miliarium slot, `phase4` and `stubs`, each entry holding an address, a nonce where one is known, and the `status` field (vii) requires so the abandoned nonces 87 to 89 stay visible rather than being silently dropped. The generator is `tools/generate_deployment_json.py`, stdlib-only Python, reading the committed deployment record and the stub topology ledger and writing `deployments/<chainId>.json`, with its audit a diff of generator output against those two sources per (viii). Two alternatives are rejected on the record rather than by silence: a Foundry script writing through `vm.writeJson` would stay entirely in-toolchain but cannot parse the record's markdown tables, so it would have to re-derive from chain and env and would break (viii) outright; shell with `awk` and `jq` adds a runtime binary this repo has never required and hand-rolls JSON quoting, which is the fragile path. `tools/` does not exist today, and creating it is a LAYOUT decision rather than a dependency one — `script/` stays Foundry deploy scripts per CLAUDE.md section 3 and the split keeps the two toolchains legible. No pip package enters the tree, `.venv` remains Slither-only so section 8c is untouched, and the generator is invoked as `python3 tools/generate_deployment_json.py` rather than through any virtualenv.

## PB-D66 — PB3.10a: the stranded-fee recovery path, a hook-side delayed entry converting fee tokens no hot-path rail can reach and depositing them to der Bodensee; P-D12's ixEDEL bridge resurrected off the hot path, B10 and the fail-safe both unchanged (user-adjudicated 2026-08-07; PB3.10a)

**(i) The custody chain has no exit, which is what makes this a design problem rather than the ops runbook P-D12 called for.** F-14 is Fixed and its fail-safe is correct: `onRegister` caches `poolBodenseeDepositToken` and `onAfterSwap` skips collect-convert-route without reverting when that cache is zero, so fees on a no-rail pool accrue in the Vault instead of bricking the swap. What P-D12 deferred as "a fee-custody item to resolve there" is harder than the phrase suggests. `AureumProtocolFeeController`'s two `withdrawProtocolFees*` entries revert `InvalidRecipient` for any recipient but the fee-routing hook, an immutable whose own NatSpec records that there is no setter and no governance path to change it, per D-D7 and B10. The hook exposes no sweep, rescue, withdraw, recover or skim. All three `route*` entries open with `safeTransferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), amount)`, pulling from the caller rather than spending the hook's balance. The single balance-reading path adds `depositToken.balanceOf(address(this))` to der Bodensee, and `depositToken` is svZCHF or sUSDS, which a no-rail pool by definition does not hold. So `02 ixAetheron`'s LST fees, once withdrawn to the only permitted recipient, are stranded permanently. That is F-22's shape exactly, a mechanism working as designed producing an irreversible state, and it differs only in having been known: P-D12 flagged it rather than missing it, and understated it.

**(ii) Three user constraints collapse the option space to one shape.** Fees must end at der Bodensee, so a permanent Vault sink is not a resolution but the same accumulation with extra steps. Nothing may accumulate anywhere else, which removes the hook itself and any operations wallet as a resting place. And the solution must be universal rather than ixAetheron-specific, because `onRegister` is permissive and nothing prevents a future pool registering with neither rail. Those three together kill the accept-and-record option outright and leave a recovery path as the only remaining shape.

**(iii) The shape: a second entry, delayed and permissioned, that spends the hook's own balance.** It is the inverse of the existing `route*` family in exactly one respect, and that inversion is the whole point: it operates on `balanceOf(address(this))` rather than pulling from `msg.sender`, because the tokens are already there and no external party holds them. It converts along a caller-supplied route and terminates in the existing `_addLiquidityOneSidedToBodenseeViaVault`, so value cannot stop short of der Bodensee. It is universal rather than pool-specific, which is what satisfies the future-pools constraint; `poolBodenseeDepositToken == address(0)` EXPLAINS why a balance is stranded and is not a runtime check, per (xii). The hot path is untouched: no conversion is added to `onAfterSwap` for no-rail pools, because the venue wall that forced the fail-safe still stands.

**(iv) The caller is `governanceModule`, reusing the seat rather than inventing an authority, and PB-D61 (ii) is amended from two gated entries to three.** `setTrustedRouter` and `routeGovernanceDeposit` already gate on that slot; it is one-shot-sealed under C-D11 and burns its own admin. Gating recovery the same way inherits an authority already adjudicated instead of adding a fourth trust surface. It also dodges F-22 by construction: gating on `AureumGovernance` would require a fourth proposal type, and omitting one would leave the entry permitted and reachable by nobody, which is the precise defect the PB-D63 and PB-D64 ladder just spent eleven rungs repairing. PB-D61 (iii)'s Stage-R rule is UNCHANGED and becomes more load-bearing, not less: the seat must hold something able to express arbitrary calls, a Safe or an EOA, and never `AureumGovernance`, because a third stranded lever now rides on that same choice.

**(v) Two steps, not one, with the invariant stated so the intermediate is not mistaken for a second sink.** `withdrawProtocolFees*` is `authenticate`-gated on the controller and recovery is `governanceModule`-gated on the hook; these are different authorities and coupling them into a single transaction would force one contract to call the other and bind two seams that have no reason to be bound. The governing invariant is that a hook-held fee-token balance is protocol-owned and convertible only through this entry, so the window between withdrawal and conversion is mechanics rather than a resting place. A fleeting intermediate is not an accumulation; a runbook that withdraws to a Safe and defers the rest is, and is rejected at (viii).

**(vi) The venue is caller-supplied POOL IDS with per-hop minimums, executed inside `Vault.unlock`, never external calldata and never an external Router.** Hops run through the existing nested-swap pattern the hook already uses, `_vault.swap` followed by settling `tokenIn` and `sendTo` for the output, so the conversion never leaves the Vault and the hook never makes a call to an address a caller chose. The distinction is the security boundary of this entire clause: caller-supplied calldata on the contract that custodies protocol fees is an arbitrary-call hatch under another name, and would be the worst surface in the system to hand an auditor, whereas a caller-supplied ordered list of pool identifiers is only a route. Bounds follow the PB2.4 and F-13 discipline already applied to `routeYieldFee`: a `minDepositTokenOut` on the conversion and a `minBptAmountOut` on the Bodensee add, both caller-supplied.

**(vii) This is P-D12's ixEDEL bridge resurrected, not a new external-DEX design, and the lock says so to stop the drop being re-litigated.** P-D12 rejected an on-chain two-hop bridge on stated grounds of multi-hop cost, a bridge registry, cross-pool coupling and doubled F-13 slippage "on the crown-jewel hot path". Every one of those grounds is hot-path-specific and none binds a permissioned, delayed entry that no user swap traverses. The route exists in the constellation already: an LST converts to ixEDEL on ixAetheron itself, and ixEDEL converts to svZCHF on any Standard pool holding both, of which there are many. A reader meeting this clause should understand that the rejection lapsed with its rationale rather than that it was overridden.

**(viii) What is rejected, on the record.** An operations EOA as destination, because it violates the no-accumulation constraint unless it is a momentary hop inside an atomic convert-and-deposit flow, and a withdraw-to-Safe-and-deal-with-it-later runbook simply recreates the strand one address further along. Loosening B10 so the recipient may be something other than the hook, because it fights D-D7 on purpose and still leaves the question of where LSTs may safely rest. Re-opening the two-hop on the hot path, which P-D12's grounds still bind. And requiring every future pool to hold svZCHF or sUSDS, which is sound policy and recorded at (ix) as such, but cannot help an already-registered ixAetheron and does not remove the need for a recovery path.

**(ix) A composition or gauge rule barring future no-rail pools is recorded and NOT built here.** It is genuine defense in depth for the future-pools constraint, and it belongs with the Stage-O composition machinery rather than with this entry. It is explicitly not a dependency: the recovery path is required regardless, because ixAetheron is already registered and `onRegister` stays permissive, so blocking the build on a policy change would delay a fix the policy cannot substitute for.

**(x) Stage-R gate, chain-independent, on the PB-D61 and RB-017 shape.** The controller immutable, the hook's surface and the stranded tokens are identical in the source Stage R will deploy, so the mainnet hook admits exactly the same strand. Sepolia carries no urgency of its own — its fee accrual is testnet-scale — but the entry is built and gated now because the constraint is a property of the source rather than of the chain.

**(xi) The ladder.** a this lock. b the recovery entry on the hook with its `governanceModule` gate, balance-spending semantics, pool-id route and terminal Bodensee add. c the unit tests, including a negative proving a non-module caller cannot reach it and a positive proving a stranded balance reaches der Bodensee. d a mainnet-fork witness over the real ixEDEL route. e the PB-D61 (ii) amendment, the RB row for the future-pools policy, the Decisions row, the Completion Log and the section 11 update.

**(xii) The recovery entry's exact signature, and why it carries no pool argument.** The entry takes no pool identifier and cannot, because the hook holds a FUNGIBLE balance per token with no memory of which pool produced it. Clause (iii)'s `poolBodenseeDepositToken == address(0)` is therefore descriptive rather than a runtime key, and (iii) is softened accordingly: it explains why a balance became stranded, and the entry never reads it. That is sound rather than a loosening, on two grounds. A non-rail balance surviving BETWEEN transactions is stranded by definition, since `onAfterSwap` opens and closes its accounting inside one transaction and leaves nothing behind on the success path. And a rail balance never needs this entry at all, because the hot path already sweeps leftover svZCHF and sUSDS opportunistically where `_swapFeeAndDeposit` adds `depositToken.balanceOf(address(this))` to der Bodensee. The signature is `recoverStrandedFees(IERC20 feeToken, IERC20 depositToken, address[] calldata swapPools, IERC20[] calldata hopTokenOuts, uint256[] calldata minHopOuts, uint256 minBptAmountOut)` returning `uint256 bptMinted`. The `hopTokenOuts` array is NOT optional decoration: `_swapExactInFeeTokenToDepositTokenViaVault` takes an explicit `tokenIn` and `tokenOut` beside its pool, and a pool address alone does not determine an output token on a multi-token pool, so the LST-to-ixEDEL hop on the five-token ixAetheron is underdetermined without it. Four invariants hold: the three arrays are equal in length; that length is greater than zero; `hopTokenOuts[last]` equals `depositToken`; and hop i takes `tokenIn` as `feeToken` when i is zero and `hopTokenOuts[i-1]` otherwise, with `tokenOut` as `hopTokenOuts[i]`. There is no amount parameter, the entry spending `feeToken.balanceOf(address(this))` in full per (iii). Four errors join the interface beside the existing shared gates: `InvalidDepositToken(address token)` when `depositToken` is neither `SV_ZCHF` nor `SUSDS`; `EmptySwapPath()`; `SwapPathLengthMismatch(uint256 pools, uint256 outs, uint256 mins)` carrying all three lengths in one revert, chosen over pairwise errors so a caller sees the whole disagreement rather than fixing one pair and meeting the next; and `TerminalTokenMismatch(address expected, address actual)`, kept distinct from `InvalidDepositToken` because "not a rail" and "the route does not end where you said" are different faults and conflating them costs diagnosis. `UnsupportedFeeToken` stays implementation-local and does not move. A zero-hop fast path for the case where `feeToken` already equals `depositToken` is deliberately NOT provided: it is unreachable for the LST strand this entry exists to clear, and `EmptySwapPath` rejects it, so a later reader does not add one as a helpful optimisation.

## PB-D67 — PB3.5i10: explorer verification is rung i's unfinished half rather than a new sub-step; 50 of 50 targeted contracts verified, three skipped as source-drifted and three as abandoned (user-adjudicated 2026-08-07; PB3.5i10)

**(i) The rung this belongs to, and the status claim it corrects.** PB-D27 (x) defines rung i as the live broadcast, the regenerated stub map, and explorer verification, with rung j as the Completion Log plus the section 11 advance. Rungs i8 and i9 closed the broadcast alone, after which section 11 recorded PB3.5 COMPLETE. That claim was premature by rung i's own definition: verification had never run and rung j had never been reached. The Surfaces table at `docs/STAGE_P_BIS_PLAN.md` L32 names the surface "Sepolia broadcast + explorer verification" against PB3.5, so no new sub-step number is created here and none is needed — the work resumes at i10 and closes at j. The blocker was never technical. `ETHERSCAN_API_KEY` existed as an empty slot in all three environment files, and an operator supplied the value.

**(ii) The roster is 50, and the arithmetic against PB-D65's 51 is load-bearing.** PB-D65 measured 51 live protocol contracts as nine base-layer plus twenty-six pools plus sixteen phase-4. This rung verified 50: that 51, minus the three phase-4 contracts clause (iv) skips, plus the two clause (v) contracts the 51 never counted. The two movements are independent and must not be read as cancelling — one is a deliberate exclusion of live contracts, the other a correction to the inventory itself.

**(iii) Nonces 87 to 89 stay unverified, by decision.** The abandoned pre-EIP-170 base layer is permanently orphaned per PB-D32: `AureumVaultFactory` at nonce 89 seals a creation-code hash for a Vault that overran the limit and cannot be deployed on any chain. Deployment-record section 1 already labels the set ABANDONED in prose, which is what a reader arriving from the explorer needs; verified source for contracts nothing will ever call adds nothing a label does not. The two survivors at 87 and 88 are bytecode-identical to their live successors at 90 and 91, so a reader who wants their source has it.

**(iv) `TVLOracle`, `AureumGovernance` and `AureumGovernanceAuthorizer` stay unverified because the source moved after they were deployed.** All three were broadcast on 2026-08-02 and all three have behavioral commits landing after: the oracle took `870fc2f`, `0eec0d4` and `36dadc3` for the PB-D50 venue-ratio rebuild, `AureumGovernance` took `1fc0c66` for F-21 and `4e15d3c` plus `a8cfa8b` for F-22, and `AureumGovernanceAuthorizer` took `5bef424` for the F-22 emergency-set widening. The oracle case is not arguable rather than merely likely: forge's own broadcast artifact records six constructor arguments against the eight the current constructor takes. Verifying any of the three would require building at the deploy-time revision, which publishes source carrying F-21 and F-22 on a public explorer while both are fixed in the tree. All three are scheduled for redeploy at Stage R from corrected source, where they verify from HEAD without ceremony. This is the same posture PB-D52 (viii) took toward the live oracle and is recorded here so the gap reads as a decision rather than an omission.

**(v) `VaultAdmin` and `VaultExtension` are live, were in no artifact, and are now verified.** Nonce 93's `factory.create()` CALL deploys four contracts rather than one. Deployment-record section 1 says so in prose but records only the Vault's address, and `deployments/11155111.json` carries neither sibling. They are `VaultAdmin` at `0xb5e5690cdba0f0c41217cfa361ac424d1e356eb5` and `VaultExtension` at `0xac58bab6a4ad5019d3725eb456531eae96550d44`, holding 12,405 and 20,069 bytes of deployed code against the Vault's own 24,393, and the Vault delegatecalls into both, so the majority of its logic lived at addresses no committed artifact named. Neither was recoverable from a nonce, both having been created inside a CALL. They were identified by matching each candidate's recorded init code against the creation code of the two artifacts, which is a positive identification rather than a guess by elimination. This is RB-014's class exactly — a deploy path creating more contracts than any record names — recurring on the Vault after being closed for the stubs. The fourth sibling is the 17-byte CREATE3 proxy, carrying no source worth publishing, and each of the 27 CREATE3 pools has one.

**(vi) Verification was gated behind two local checks, and neither cost an API call.** Submitting 50 contracts blind and reading the failures would have been the cheaper thing to write and the more expensive thing to run. Instead the deployed-code length of every target was compared against its local artifact, which settled the Router profile question by measurement and identified the two Vault siblings; then every target's constructor arguments were extracted by stripping the artifact's creation code off the init code forge recorded, which both produced exact arguments and proved byte-exact creation-code identity, since creation code carries no immutables and must therefore be an exact prefix. All 50 returned PREFIX-OK with argument lengths a multiple of 32. The pre-flight also confirmed what would otherwise have been asserted: `bytecode_hash = "none"` with `cbor_metadata = false` means an uncommitted interface edit adding four unused errors left `AureumFeeRoutingHook` byte-identical, which is the PB15 rule applied rather than quoted.

**(vii) The 26 pools returned already-verified, and that is evidence rather than a shortcut.** Etherscan matches at the runtime-bytecode layer, so der Bodensee's `WeightedPool` submission in the base-layer group linked all 26 Miliarium pools, which share that source and differ only in immutables. The response forge reports is address-keyed rather than heuristic, and it is corroborated independently: each pool's own constructor arguments were extracted from its own creation trace and confirmed PREFIX-OK against its own address before any submission. Slot 02 included, whose third-sender broadcast is recorded in section 10.

**(viii) The interface edit was stashed for the hook's submission, and the reason is ABI fidelity rather than bytecode.** Unused errors emit no code, so the hook verifies identically with the PB-D66 working-tree edit present. Inherited errors do enter the ABI, so verifying with a dirty tree would publish an ABI advertising four errors the deployed hook cannot revert with. The edit was stashed for the base-layer group and restored after.

**(ix) The two profiles share one cache file, which broke the Router and becomes RB-018.** `[profile.vault]` overrides `out` but not `cache_path`, so `cache/solidity-files-cache.json` records whichever build ran last. A vault build immediately before verification rewrote every `pkg/vault/contracts` entry to 0.8.30, and the Router — the only vault-package file the DEFAULT profile deploys — then failed lookup with "No matching artifact found for Router with compiler version 0.8.26" while a correct 0.8.26 artifact sat in `out/`. A default rebuild restored the entry and the submission passed. The general form is that two profiles writing one cache make build ORDER load-bearing for any file both compile, silently and with an error naming a version rather than an ordering. Stage R verifies a mainnet Router from the default profile against the same shared cache and reproduces this by construction.

**(x) The ladder.** i10 this lock. i11 the plan-side Decisions row. i12 deployment-record section 1, the two sibling addresses and the base-layer verification status. i13 sections 7 and 10, the pools. i14 sections 11 through 14, the phase-4 status and the four remaining blocked-on-key sentences. i15 `deployments/11155111.json`, the two additions. i16 the runbook, prerequisite 3 and phase 6. i17 the RB-018 row. j the Completion Log rows and the section 11 advance, closing PB3.5 on the definition it was always held to.

## PB-D68 — PB3.11a: F-23, the fee-routing hook mints redeemable BPT where the value-capture model requires irreversible depth; severity High, exposure zero and latent, and the fix forces a full Sepolia refresh with a new genesis (user-adjudicated 2026-08-07; PB3.11a)

**(i) The defect.** `AureumFeeRoutingHook._addLiquidityOneSidedToBodenseeViaVault` deposits into der Bodensee with `AddLiquidityKind.UNBALANCED` and `to: address(this)`, minting BPT to the hook for every protocol fee routed. BPT is a redeemable claim. The canonical spec at `04_tokenomics.md` requires the opposite and says so twice: "No wei of AuMM, svZCHF, or sUSDS is transferred out", and der Bodensee framed as "every swap fee deepens the lake" with "no buyback / burn / market-purchase logic anywhere in the protocol". Depth that carries a claim is not irreversible depth. The protocol's own pattern elsewhere is correct and explicit: `BodenseeBootstrapChannel._distributeCallback` and `SwapAndDepositToBodensee` both add with `AddLiquidityKind.DONATION` and both revert `BptMintedOnDonation(uint256 bptOut)` if a single wei of BPT appears. Der Bodensee is created with `enableDonation = true`, so donation was available to the hook from the first block.

**(ii) Two economic consequences, one of which is the finding and one of which is not.** AuMM still reprices correctly under either mechanism, because weighted-pool spot price is a function of rate-scaled balances and weights rather than of BPT supply; that half of the spec's mechanism was never at risk. What differs is ownership and reversibility. A donation raises every existing LP's claim on a deeper pool. An UNBALANCED add mints new BPT that dilutes them proportionally, leaving LPs flat and handing the increment to the hook as a claim on exactly the depth just added. That claim is currently unexercisable only because the hook exposes no redeem, sweep or transfer path, which is the same absence PB-D66 (i) traced for the LST strand. The guarantee therefore rests on an omission rather than on a construction, and any future hook revision that adds a token-moving path makes the lake drainable.

**(iii) How it survived four stages, which is why the lock records the provenance.** The Stage-D resolution at `docs/STAGE_D_NOTES.md` L225-L235 debated Router versus direct Vault call from inside a hook callback and nothing else; `kind: UNBALANCED` arrived inherited from the original plan's `IRouter.addLiquiditySingleTokenExactIn`, which mints BPT by construction, and was never itself examined. OQ-2 governs the swap TARGET and der Bodensee's exclusion from skimming, not the claim. `docs/STAGE_D_NOTES.md` L132 records unbalanced-liquidity operations as enabled precisely because the fee router needs them. At no point does any record ask whether protocol fees should carry a claim at all. The question was not decided wrongly; it was never put.

**(iv) Severity High, exposure zero and latent, both measured rather than assumed.** High because the defect sits on the crown-jewel value-capture path and contradicts the protocol's stated core mechanism, and because the safety of the outcome depends on an absent function rather than an enforced invariant. Exposure is nil: der Bodensee's BPT `totalSupply` reads 0 on Sepolia and the hook's BPT balance reads 0, so no add of any kind has ever completed and nothing needs unwinding. Sepolia also cannot exercise the yield leg at all, because `StubERC4626.convertToAssets` is `pure` and returns its argument, a frozen one-to-one NAV that never accrues; the PoC and the fix's proof are therefore mainnet-fork work against real rate providers.

**(v) The fix, mirroring the working precedent rather than inventing a shape.** `_addLiquidityOneSidedToBodenseeViaVault` becomes a donation on the `BodenseeBootstrapChannel._distributeCallback` pattern: transfer the deposit token to the Vault, `settle`, then `addLiquidity` with `kind: AddLiquidityKind.DONATION`, then assert `bptOut == 0`, then assert the pool reserve rose by exactly the donated amount. The accounting order INVERTS relative to today's code and that is load-bearing, not stylistic: a donation mints no BPT credit to offset the debit, so credit must exist before the add. All four consumers inherit the change through the shared helper — `onAfterSwap`, `routeYieldFee`, `routeGovernanceDeposit` and `routeIncendiaryDeposit`. The last two are dormant with zero callers, and their live counterparts in Stage K and Stage L already donate through `SwapAndDepositToBodensee`, so donating here aligns the dormant rails with the economics their live equivalents already implement rather than changing any behaviour in flight.

**(vi) `bptMinted` is KEPT and asserted zero rather than removed.** The return values and the `bptMinted` field on `SwapFeeRouted`, `YieldFeeRouted`, `GovernanceDepositRouted` and `IncendiaryDepositRouted` become constant zero. Keeping them preserves the ABI for any indexer, costs nothing, and converts a silent constant into a checked invariant that matches the `BptMintedOnDonation` discipline the two sibling subsystems already enforce. Removing them would break external decoders for no economic gain.

**(vii) G-D11 is SUPERSEDED, explicitly and not by contradiction.** Its language sanctioning an UNBALANCED fee path coexisting with DONATION is the sentence that made this defect look intentional to every subsequent reader. A lock that merely disagrees with it leaves two live decisions pointing opposite ways, so G-D11's coexistence clause is retired here by name and the F-23 fix is the sole governing shape.

**(viii) Sepolia is refreshed in full, sequenced as (C) then (A), because a hook cannot be swapped under a live constellation.** Redeploying the hook alone is not available: the Vault seals `_hooksContracts[pool]` at registration with no production setter, `AureumProtocolFeeController.FEE_ROUTING_HOOK` is immutable, and F-22's accepted residual leaves `setProtocolFeeController` unreachable by design. A corrected hook is therefore only reachable by redeploying everything that points at it. The order is: defer PB3.9; land the F-23 source fix; rebuild the Sepolia constellation; seed the corrected constellation; start the new clock. PB3.9 must not seed der Bodensee under the defective hook, and the urgency is precise rather than dramatic: PB3.9 itself donates through `BodenseeBootstrapChannel` and mints no fee BPT, but it takes `totalSupply` above zero, after which the FIRST successful fee route realises F-23 on a live defective hook.

**(ix) The cascade's real price is the genesis reset, not gas.** The whole constellation cost roughly 0.2 SepETH across about 690 transactions against a deployer holding 5.72, so funding is noise and more is mineable. The binding cost is the emission clock. `AuMM.setMinter` is one-shot and burns `_minterAdmin` to zero on use, so AuMM is permanently sealed to the current `AuMMMinterRouter`; that router exposes exactly one external function, `mintFor`, holds `AUMM`, `BOOTSTRAP_CHANNEL` and `EMISSION_DISTRIBUTOR` as immutables, and has no setter of any kind. A refresh deploys a new distributor and bootstrap channel, the old router cannot be re-pointed at them, and AuMM cannot be re-pointed at a new router. AuMM therefore redeploys, `GENESIS_BLOCK` 11477620 is abandoned, and the new genesis is `broadcast_block + 100_800` per PB-D19 — roughly fourteen days from whenever the new base layer broadcasts. The 87 testnet stubs survive as plain tokens; base layer, all 26 Miliarium pools, der Bodensee, both factories, the Router, all 16 phase-4 contracts and AuMM do not.

**(x) Live Sepolia is observer-canonical only AFTER the refresh.** The current constellation is abandoned for value-capture purposes the moment F-23 is accepted, and this sentence exists so that status cannot be softened later into "mostly fine for now". Sepolia is the showcase; a chain demonstrating a value-capture mechanism the protocol has already rejected is worse than a chain with nothing deployed, because it teaches observers the wrong invariant.

**(xi) Two follow-ons named here and adjudicated separately.** First, batching: if Sepolia is rebuilt anyway, the refresh should also carry PB-D52 (viii)'s deliberately-unfixed `TVLOracle` and F-22's unremediated Sepolia governance stack, since both were declined on cascade cost that this refresh now pays regardless, and skipping them buys a third restart later. Second, the seal chain: AuMM to router to distributor is unrotatable by construction, so on mainnet any emission-layer defect found after deployment carries this same full-restart unwind. That belongs in the backport register as a Stage-R awareness row rather than inside F-23, because it is a property of the emission layer rather than of the fee path.

**(xii) The ladder.** a this lock. b the mainnet-fork PoC proving BPT mints on a fee route where the spec forbids a claim. c the helper fix with its donation ordering, zero-BPT assertion and reserve-delta check. d the test re-gate plus the PB-D66 (iii) amendment retargeting the PB3.10 recovery path at donation and unpausing b2b. e the F-23 ledger row, the G-D11 supersession, the RB row for the seal chain, the Decisions row, the Completion Log and the section 11 update.

**(xiii) Clause (i)'s `to: address(this)` describes one rail of four, and clause (ii)'s safety argument is correspondingly narrower (user-adjudicated 2026-08-08; PB3.11c pre-flight).** `_swapFeeAndDeposit` takes a `bptRecipient` argument at L479 and forwards it to the helper's `to` field at L503; only `onAfterSwap` supplies the hook itself, at L376. The three `IAureumFeeRoutingHook` entries pass `caller` — L653 for `routeYieldFee`, L701 for `routeGovernanceDeposit`, L749 for `routeIncendiaryDeposit` — so yield-fee BPT mints to `AureumProtocolFeeController` and the two dormant rails mint to the governance and incendiary modules. Clause (ii) argues the claim is unexercisable because the hook exposes no redeem, sweep or transfer path; that argument holds for the swap rail alone, and for the other three the holder is a different contract whose paths are its own to audit. Exposure does not move — der Bodensee's BPT `totalSupply` is 0, no add of any kind has completed, and nothing needs unwinding — so severity stays High on clause (iv)'s reasoning rather than rising. Recorded as an appended correction rather than an edit to (i) so the original reading stays legible to a reader tracing how the defect was first characterised.

**(xiv) `minBptAmountOut` is rejected nonzero, and the guard sits in the helper rather than at the three entries (user-adjudicated 2026-08-08; PB3.11c pre-flight).** `AddLiquidityKind.DONATION` returns `bptOut == 0` by construction, so a caller-supplied BPT floor is unsatisfiable at any nonzero value and the phase-2 half of F-13's fix protects a mint leg that no longer exists. The parameter stays on all three external entries, because clause (vi)'s ABI argument applies to inputs exactly as it does to returns; the helper passes literal `0` to the Vault and reverts `BptFloorUnavailableOnDonation(uint256 minBptAmountOut)` on any nonzero value. Placement is in the helper beside the DONATION call rather than at the entries: it sits with the invariant it protects, one site instead of three, and a future entry point cannot forget it — the cost is that a nonzero floor reverts only after the phase-1 swap leg has run, which is acceptable because no correct caller passes one. Forwarding the value instead would brick the route through the Vault's own `BptAmountOutBelowMin`, a revert naming a bound satisfiable at no value; dropping it silently would leave a slippage parameter a caller believes protects them and does not, the precise failure class F-13 was raised about and the one clause (vi) already rejected on the return side. F-13's bounded-route protection therefore rests entirely on `minDepositTokenOut`, and that is a narrowing of scope rather than of strength: `test_Fork_RouteYieldFee_minDepositTokenOut_revertsWhenTooTight` survives the fix untouched as the positive witness, while `test_Fork_RouteYieldFee_minBptAmountOut_revertsWhenTooTight` changes expected selector from `IVaultErrors.BptAmountOutBelowMin` to the new error and both realistic-bound tests lose their premise outright.

**(xv) `bptRecipient` is retired rather than left inert (user-adjudicated 2026-08-08; PB3.11c pre-flight).** It is not part of the external ABI — each entry derives it from `msg.sender` inside its own `unlock` payload — so removing it costs no compatibility, and the helper passes `to: address(this)` exactly as the precedent does. `_swapFeeAndDeposit` drops from seven parameters to six, the argument leaves the helper signature, and the four call sites at L376, L653, L701 and L749 each lose one argument. The reason to remove rather than keep is clause (iii)'s: a live-looking recipient parameter on a path that mints nothing is the kind of surface that let an UNBALANCED kind read as intentional to four stages of readers, and leaving it would preserve exactly that misreading for the next one.

**(xvi) Three mechanical consequences, recorded so rung c's diff is not read as scope creep (user-adjudicated 2026-08-08; PB3.11c pre-flight).** First, error placement follows this file pair's existing split rather than the precedent's single location: `BptFloorUnavailableOnDonation` is caller-triggerable input validation and joins the route-level errors in `IAureumFeeRoutingHook` beside `ZeroAmount` and `InvalidPool`, while `BptMintedOnDonation(uint256 bptOut)` and `ReserveDeltaMismatch(uint256 expected, uint256 actual)` are internal invariants no caller can reach by input and are declared on the contract beside `UnsupportedFeeToken`, with names and argument shapes copied verbatim from `BodenseeBootstrapChannel` L94 and L97 so the two subsystems stay greppable as one discipline. Second, the settle source changes: today's helper transfers and settles `amountsIn[depositIndex]`, the Vault's returned consumed amount, and the NatSpec at L551—L563 prescribes both that convention and the `addLiquidity`-then-settle ordering as deliberate. A donation settles before the add, so that tuple member does not exist yet, the transfer uses `depositAmount`, `amountsIn` becomes a discard, and all three of those NatSpec sentences — the ordering, the caller-parameterized `minBptAmountOut`, and the settle source — are rewritten rather than left standing, with the reserve-delta assertion replacing the third as the guarantee that the Vault consumed exactly what was settled. Third, the unit suites barely move and that is a property of their mocks rather than of the fix: the three payload-encoding tests in `test/unit/AureumFeeRoutingHook.t.sol` mock `_vault.unlock` and the four bound-forwarding tests in `test/unit/AureumProtocolFeeController.t.sol` mock the hook, so none enters the callback and none fails; their nonzero mocked `bptMinted` returns become values the system can no longer produce and are corrected to zero on clause (vi)'s discipline rather than because they broke. Clause (xii)'s rung c is amended to the full scope: the ordering inversion, both assertions, the input guard, the parameter retirement and the NatSpec rewrite land as one save.
